[^40]: **The opening hour is damaged.** S’s opening poem says 庚寅, Gengyin; its general explanation and all six entries concern 戊寅, Wuyin, as does G617’s opening. “[Wuyin]” follows that supported repair. The Gengzi entry’s 純申, pure Shen, is retained despite G’s 純甲: the former is a possible reading and is not replaced simply because the witnesses differ.

[^41]: **A surname and a poetic word restored.** S’s 傳伯壽 is G617’s 傅伯壽, supporting “[Fu] Boshou.” S’s 顯憂華 is G’s 顯榮華, supporting “[splendor].” The ominous and favorable verdicts attached to different conditions remain intact; a repaired graph does not authorize harmonizing the whole section.

[^42]: **Embryo and an uncertain construction.** 胎生元命 is explained through Geng receiving Embryo at Mao, not through calculating a separate conception pillar. S and G617 both transmit 若柱旺丁生月帶祿者貴. The English punctuates this as flourishing pillars, Ding giving generation, and the month carrying Lu; the sequence is compressed and the witnesses do not secure that division uniquely. S’s 中未 in the small verse is read temporally with G’s 中末, middle and later life.

[^43]: **A damaged name recovered.** S has 萬⿱尚書, an incomplete character-description sign after Wan. G617 gives 萬崙, supporting Wan “[Lun].” In the Gengshen catalogue S’s chief councillor has Jimao/Jiayin, while G has Jimao/Bingyin. S’s valid month is retained rather than changed to produce an identical catalogue.

[^44]: **Metal–Fire in the poem, Metal–Water in the prose.** S’s opening reads 金火, whereas G617 reads 金水. The translation retains S’s Fire in the verse and Water in the prose. The subsequent distinction between the two Kuigang days and the other four Geng days is explicit and essential; their opposite preferences for Wealth and Officer should not be collapsed into one rule.

[^45]: **Three Kuigang pillars, not four.** The Gengchen/Gengchen year-month pair occurs under Gengzi day and Gengchen hour, yielding three Gengchen pillars. The text’s “three” is therefore retained. Its description of the robber’s punishment as fitting is the source’s moral verdict. Guo Xiyan’s additional remonstrance and execution caption, including “How grievous!”, is also preserved; the translator is not supplying that exclamation independently.

[^46]: **Incomplete titles and different names.** S gives 王鼎, Wang Ding, where G617 has 黃鼎; S’s surname remains. 張佳𦙍 is rendered Zhang Jiayin; G writes 張佳引. G makes the shared censor’s chart explicit, but its additional personal name is not imported. S leaves an isolated 司 after that caption, which is not expanded into an invented office. 王麟洲少□ has an incomplete junior title, expressly identified as such. The Yihai/Gengchen case’s Jiaxu major luck, Wuchen year, Jiwei minor luck, and age fifty-four are separate components of the death annotation.

[^47]: **A name fragment and an extraordinary age.** S prints 未清霞道人, G617 末清霞道人. The uncertain first graph is not silently turned into a known surname; the main text identifies the uncertainty and keeps Qingxia. The stated 120 years remains a catalogue claim, not an authenticated lifespan. The Yiwei/Gengchen case describes its subject’s son Huang Shijun; it does not itself establish whether the chart belongs to the mother or father.

[^48]: **Punctuation leaves a real ambiguity.** S, G617, and U have 不通無貴壽考. The English reads 不通無貴，壽考: without communication there is no distinction, but longevity. An alternative division allows 無 to govern distinction and long life together. The consulted witnesses do not securely determine the punctuation; the favorable lifespan reading is an interpretation, not a recovered missing conjunction.

[^49]: **An expressly attributed hour disagreement.** The same Jiashen/Bingzi year-month pair is given for Zhang Xueyan under Gengchen/Xinsi as 原本, original text, and under Gengchen/Renwu as 星案, *Star Cases*. Neither record is discarded. Their different hours should not be mistaken for two interchangeable labels of one complete chart.

[^50]: **Earth restored; Mao retained.** S’s 上木運 is G617’s 土木運, supporting “[Earth]-and-Wood luck.” In the preceding monthly list, however, S has Si, Wu, and Mao while G has Si, Wu, and Chou. S’s possible Mao is kept. The distinction between a damaged graph and a substantive alternative reading matters.

[^51]: **Food without qi, yet flourishing in a later entry.** The general account says Ren Food lacks qi at Wu; the Gengchen entry says Food God flourishes. Both occur in S and G617 and remain. Likewise, Fire qi is cautioned against generally but gives great distinction in the Gengzi entry. No unexpressed condition has been supplied to harmonize these judgments. 發即死, development immediately followed by death, is kept without medical diagnosis or a calculated time of death.

[^52]: **A valid name difference and an unequal shared-chart outcome.** S has 呂本, Lü Ben, at Renwu hour; the earlier alternative-hour notice names 李本, Li Ben. The two readings remain identifiable. S also contrasts the Zhejiang grand secretary with an ordinary man from Jiangning sharing the chart. G617 omits that comparison, but this is not grounds to delete it. The Gengwu Jisi/Gengwu prefect differs from G’s Jisi/Jiawu; S’s valid pair remains.

[^53]: **A broken line and an incomplete title.** The Hong Chengxuan caption divides 癸丑 across lines: Guichou is his metropolitan examination year, not a second natal month. 未廷試卒 means death before the palace examination. The Xinchou/Jiawu case ends in 甲部, a damaged ministry designation that neither G617 nor the surrounding S establishes. Its office is not silently expanded to Personnel, War, or another ministry.

[^54]: **A conjectural passive.** S, G617, and U all have 官星魁制. The sense required by the next explanation is Officer restrained by Gui; the translation supplies “[restrained]” while treating 魁 as probably damaged for 被 or another connective. This is a conjectural reading, not a graphic variant attested by the consulted witnesses. The Ji/Si confusions in the prose are clear from Ji Earth restraining Gui and are confirmed by U’s 己.

[^55]: **Names are not normalized across witnesses.** S’s 曽于拱 is Zeng Yugong; G617 gives 曾干拱. S’s 汪𤣥錫 is Wang Xuanxi, whereas G writes 元錫. Both differences remain accounted for without replacing S’s names. The princely confinement, restoration, and count of thirty-six sons are transmitted catalogue statements, not independently established histories or biological predictions.

[^56]: **Sources and an ethical question within the catalogue.** S names 史記, 家語, and 路史 while presenting different year/month accounts for Confucius. The translation preserves that compressed citation without declaring either chart historically proved. Its question about righteous men sharing his birth remains the annotator’s question. The Xu Qigao and twins captions are kept as separate cases, and the wording does not authorize supplying the twins’ missing names.

[^57]: **Wuchen restored.** S gives 戊長 beneath the Jisi senior provincial administrator; G617 gives 戊辰. “[Wuchen]” follows the attested correction. The bare Gengyin/Yiyou entry has no caption in S: G supplies a prefect’s name, but that additional identification is not imported.

[^58]: **The compiler corrects a rigid prohibition.** The text explicitly argues that hard Metal needs Fire, cites seven methods of Returning Lu, and rejects blanket exclusion of Si, Wu, and Xu. The general prohibition and the corrective discussion are both translated. S’s Gengyin/Jiashen title 上郎 is damaged; it is left as an uncertain official title. The contrast between Shi Xing and an impoverished scholar under identical pillars is retained, including the source’s regional distinction.

[^59]: **Two repeated pairs are not duplicates to discard.** Dinghai/Guimao is given for an education commissioner and for Minister Cai Guozhen. The Wu Dui/licentiate comparison likewise records two people sharing one chart. 沈㴶 is read Shen Que, retaining the source’s rare graph rather than substituting an assumed personal identity. The small verse’s 財稂 is supported by G617’s 財糧, hence “[grain].”

[^60]: **A troublesome line remains tentative.** 貴賤舉失胸襟 is transmitted by S and G617 and has the same wording in U. The English takes 舉 as all and 胸襟 as composure or breadth of heart: “High or low, they lose their composure.” That is a contextual interpretation, not a secure repaired Chinese reading. The preceding 不遇亥子克妻, without Hai and Zi the wife is injured, is retained rather than changing the negative to reconcile other judgments.

[^61]: **Flourishing Seal restored.** S has 印卯月, which would anomalously put Seal at Mao here; G617 has 印旺月, supporting “[flourishing] Seal.” S’s earlier 丙火戊土合局, Bing Fire and Wu Earth forming a configuration, differs from G’s 丙火戌上合局; S remains. The bowel symptoms are historical medical categories, not a diagnosis derivable from the chart.

[^62]: **The compiler’s own chart and a geographic explanation.** Wan Yuwu is the appellation used for the compiler in this tradition; the comparison with Fu Jin explains their different careers and sons through Yong and Ji. It remains an attributed account, not proof of geographic causation. The concluding statement about different years and months follows a same-chart comparison in S and G617; its awkward transition is retained. The Yichou/Bingxu poor, one-eyed case has Jiayin as a death year, not another birth-year pair. G identifies a carpenter under Gengchen/Yiyou, but S leaves that caption blank and is not silently completed.

[^63]: **Titles and valid variants.** 林爌 is rendered Lin Kuang according to the transmitted graph; it is not replaced by a different minister. The Guisi/Jichou judicial case differs from G617’s Guisi/Jihai and stays as S. 例貢 denotes the contribution-based route of tribute admission, distinct from an examination degree. The closing verse’s 没年 is read in its parallel phrase 晚景 as final years; G has the conventional 末年.

[^64]: **Cultivation as a career claim.** The caption says Gu Kexue advanced through 修養, arts of cultivation. The translation does not turn this into a modern clinical qualification or independently verify its efficacy. The source’s distinction between the ordinary Geng days and the two Kuigang days continues to govern the explanation of why weak Officer can here be favorable.

[^65]: **Numbers and a mixed-sign list preserved.** The final song specifies three wives rather than merely repeated marriage; the count remains. 癸巳戊辛 is rendered as Gui, Si, Wu, and Xin, consistent with its compact exclusions, but could partly contain the complete Guisi pillar. No single interpretation is imposed as a recovered diagram. 乙巳 in the Gengyin prose is similarly read as Yi and Ji emerging among stems, where “exposed stems” determines the grammatical role of the signs.

[^66]: **Additional captions and an incomplete month.** S adds Liu Yikun, Huang Xiyin, and a Jichou-year vice minister to the Xinmao catalogue; these additions are not dropped because G617 lacks them. The vice minister’s month is only 辛木, “Xin–Wood,” not a valid sexagenary pair, and remains marked incomplete. Luo Hongxian’s chart is expressly shared by a Songjiang licentiate and a Shandong imperial clansman. Their differing identities and regions are retained without an invented common biography.

[^67]: **A supported name reading, an unresolved month.** G617’s 胡杰 supports reading S’s 胡𤇍 as Hu [Jie]. S’s Renxu-year marquis has 士子 as the month, which is not a valid pair; no replacement is established by G’s shorter catalogue. S’s Yiyou/Xinsi prefect differs from G’s Yisi/Xinsi and remains unchanged. The man and woman sharing Dingwei/Bingwu are a single shared-chart caption, not a single person or interchangeable outcome.

[^68]: **Geese and the honored hall.** 丙離鴈侣尊堂 is compressed: Bing brings separation involving the ranks of geese, an image of siblings, and 尊堂, the honored parental hall. The translation does not turn these images into further named stars. The poem’s Si and Wu are preserved along with Bing and Ding; its exclusions are not substituted for the section’s fuller conditions.

[^69]: **Bingxu restored.** S’s 丙戊 under Gengyin is malformed. G617 gives 丙戌, supporting “[Bingxu].” This is a textual repair, not a newly calculated birth date. The month lists in the two witnesses also differ: S’s Xinwei prose says Yin and Mao Wood months, whereas G includes Wei; S’s shorter list governs.

[^70]: **An imperial alternative belongs to the source.** The Shizong caption expressly supplies Wuzi as an alternative to Jichou hour. Both readings remain visible. No choice between them is represented as an independently authenticated imperial birth record.

[^71]: **An uncertain given-name graph retained in the apparatus.** S writes 史𡎊少卿死於獄: Shi, a deputy court minister, died in prison. G617 does not include this case and therefore supplies no corrective reading. The rare graph 𡎊 is preserved here rather than replaced by an assumed modern identity. The fact that a character is rare is not evidence that the person or the record is fictitious.

[^72]: **Plans, not an obituary.** S’s 立訃成家 is supported by G617’s 立計成家: laying plans to establish a household. The reading 計 is adopted. The concluding golden-coffer image belongs to the Storehouse explanation; no additional literal treasure or independent diagram is implied.

[^73]: **The golden examination list.** S’s 名標金標 repeats 標, whereas G617 has 名標金榜. “[Examination list]” follows that supported repair. Si, Wei, and Chou are read as separate monthly branches, not an impossible Jìwèi–Chou sequence. The subsequent forecast of a wind disorder remains a historical category, not a modern diagnosis or the translation’s medical advice.

[^74]: **A difficult marriage image.** 鴛侣中年迷鏡, transmitted also by G617, combines the mandarin-pair image of marriage with a clouded or lost mirror. “Lose their mirror’s clarity” preserves the image without claiming a specific recovered event such as divorce or a spouse’s death. The final song welcomes winter birth after identifying several Water signs as diminishing blessing; neither clause is deleted to produce an unconditional rule.

[^75]: **Two textual accounts and two surnames.** Under Xinyou/Xinmao, S gives 花應期, Hua Yingqi, and cites 星案; under Xinhai/Xinmao it gives 范應期, Fan Yingqi, and cites 原本. Both use Dinghai/Xinhai year and month and mention hanging. The different name-graph and day are retained rather than silently reconciled. The judgments about Yan Shifan and Zhang Juzheng are the source’s political and prognostic commentary, not new historical findings.

[^76]: **A substantive negative retained.** S has 丑未无吉, Chou and Wei not auspicious; G617 reads 丑未尤吉, particularly auspicious. S’s valid negative remains. The short verse also differs: S gives 時月, hour and month, while G gives 皎月, the bright moon. The translation follows S rather than manufacturing a uniform poetic image from the parallel.

[^77]: **Fire and Wood, not Water and Wood.** S’s closing 火木運中通泰 differs from G617’s 水木運中通泰. Both are intelligible, so Fire-and-Wood luck is retained as S’s reading. Yin and Horse in the opening line are not silently combined into a single named Traveling-Horse position; the compact wording supplies no further diagram.

[^78]: **A month repaired; an examination title preserved.** S’s 丁丑 graduate has 丙丁 as month; G617 supplies 丙午, supporting “[Bingwu].” Both witnesses call Luo Qinshun 狀元 here. That title is translated as a catalogue claim rather than corrected from an assumed biography. S’s 貴重輕髙 differs from G’s 貴重清高; the English conveys elevated standing without declaring the transmitted sequence a securely established technical term.

[^79]: **An unrepaired surname and biographical dates.** S has 閏汝梅, rendered Run Rumei; the corresponding additional case is absent from G617. The uncommon surname graph is not silently changed to 閔 or another plausible name. Dingyou and Wuchen within the caption are examination years; Jiaxu/Gengwu remains the natal year/month pair. The final Bingyin/Renchen example is Zhang Juzheng’s mother, not the grand secretary himself.

[^80]: **The source rejects its own received charts.** The note expressly appeals to Daoist books, the *History of the Song*, and Wei Ye’s birthday poem to challenge the astrologers’ accounts of Lü Chunyang and Kou Zhun. Both the catalogue charts and the challenge are translated. 中元, Mid-Prime, is the seventh-month festival invoked by the poem; its “tomorrow” explains the preceding fourteenth-day claim. These are the source’s arguments, not independently authenticated natal dates supplied by this translation.

[^81]: **Two men, two months.** S’s flattened caption gives Hu Chaochen Jiaxu/Jiaxu and Shen Shu Jiaxu/Jisi, then says both languished in prison. G617 explicitly distinguishes Shen’s Jisi month, although its Hu entry itself has a damaged 甲戊. The English preserves S’s valid year/month pairs and does not treat the second as a biographical date.

[^82]: **An age variant.** S says 壽六旬, a lifespan of sixty; G617 says 壽不六旬, not reaching sixty. S’s age is retained. Likewise, the additional Wuxu/Jiwei ruined-household case in S is not omitted because it is absent from the shorter comparison catalogue.

[^83]: **Transformation and compact signs.** The general prose explicitly makes Gui combine with Wu and transform into Fire; ordinary Water/Fire relations alone do not express that claim. 赤白文章 retains red-and-white imagery for literary brilliance. The song’s 壬癸庚申 is translated as Ren, Gui, Geng, and Shen, but its last two characters may also be read together as Gengshen. It gives no fuller syntax that would settle the application independently.

[^84]: **An accusation reported in a case caption.** 行封侯令 describes a drinking-game command involving the bestowal of titles; a monk then denounces the man for plotting rebellion. The translation distinguishes the game, accusation, and execution rather than asserting that the chart proves a conspiracy. 棄市 is execution in the marketplace, not simple banishment. Xue Wenqing is the source’s appellation; it is not expanded into an unprinted personal biography.

[^85]: **Ghost embryo and Metal’s ruin.** 鬼胎 is retained as “ghost’s embryo” in the technical verse. Nothing here establishes a supernatural pregnancy or a clinical diagnosis. The prose calls Wu a Noble Person yet warns that the same hour may be harmful: a favorable subsidiary indicator is not allowed to erase the stated imbalance.

[^86]: **The catalogue’s unequal outcomes remain.** S contrasts Minister Chen Shaoru of Nanhai with an Imperial Academy student from Jiangxi under the same complete chart. Both are retained. The names 胡汝霖 and 沈儆价 are rendered Hu Rulin and Shen Jingjie from S; no conjectured historical identity or missing career is inserted.

[^87]: **Name, contribution, and hereditary favor.** 歐陽紳納指揮 is read as Ouyang Shen followed by 納指揮, obtaining the command through contribution, rather than making 納 part of a two-character given name. G617 preserves the same sequence. Promotion, death in battle, hereditary guard-command favor, and a metropolitan-graduate son are separate pieces of the caption; the exact recipient of the hereditary appointment is not further specified in S.

[^88]: **A doctrinal tension, not a silent correction.** S and G617 both say that visible Yi restrains hidden Ding and Ji. Ordinary Wood generates Fire rather than controlling it, so the sentence cannot simply be treated as a uniform elementary control-cycle rule. It remains as transmitted. The closing 鴛幃, bridal curtain, is marriage imagery, not a separate named chart configuration.

[^89]: **Xu restored from the parallel.** S’s monthly list has 寅午戊巳未; G617 has 寅午戌巳未. “[Xu]” follows the attested 戌. The opening 金水 and the later Bing–Xin transformation statement are retained without reducing all references to one ordinary phase assignment.

[^90]: **Discordant combination remains an uneasy reading.** 金水乖合 occurs in both S and G617. “Join discordantly” keeps 乖 rather than silently replacing it with a favorable adjective. The subsequent transformation-into-Water judgment is separately retained. Lu Shusheng’s ninety-seven years are an additional S annotation, not a lifespan inferred from the rule about winter birth.

[^91]: **Equivalent directional vocabularies.** S ends with west, south, and east; G617 uses Dui, Li, and Zhen. The translation follows S’s plain directions. These are chiefly luck-region terms here, not an instruction to travel to particular countries. The final qualification about correctly fixing the birth hour is part of the source and is not a claim that the translation has independently verified the example charts.

[^92]: **“No Ding” within a Dingyou section.** S and G617 both retain 無丁 in the general prose and 無丙丁 in several individual entries, although Ding is already the stated hour stem. This may intend no additional exposed Fire, but that qualification is not supplied by the text. The translation preserves the problem instead of silently adding “elsewhere” or changing the hour.

[^93]: **A conjectural year repair.** S has 丁害 for Shu Hua’s year; this is not a sexagenary pair. “[Dinghai]” reads 害 as the similar-sounding 亥. The case is absent from the consulted G617 catalogue, so the repair is explicitly conjectural, not claimed as an attested variant. The Renzi month and ministerial title are intact in S.

[^94]: **A birth-year dispute inside the caption.** S and G617 both give Dai Dabin a Jiyou/Renshen chart, then say that he himself reported Gengxu birth and question its attachment to Zhu Wengong’s Jiyou. Both the transmitted chart and the objection remain. The short lament 不祿 records his death, without supplying an age or a cause absent from this caption.

[^95]: **Different examination names and a contradictory child verdict.** S’s Bingwu/Jihai man is described as taking examinations under three names; the translation does not combine them into a single modern personal name. The next Jihai/Xinwei prefect is 無子, without sons, in S, but 子貴, with distinguished sons, in G617. S’s negative is retained; G’s supplied personal name is not imported.

[^96]: **Hall of Lu is not ordinary Lu residence.** S calls Xu 祿堂, Hall of Lu, not simply Xin’s regular Lu branch. The special designation is therefore preserved rather than corrected to You. The general rule’s 難為祖業 remains difficult ancestral inheritance, not an unconditional promise of inherited wealth. S’s 戊上 in the next clause is read 戊土 with G617.

[^97]: **Two supported repairs with different graphical causes.** S’s 申戌 censor-year is G617’s 甲戌, supporting “[Jiaxu].” The Xinwei-year provincial graduate’s 甲戊 month is G617’s 甲午, supporting “[Jiawu].” Neither malformed S sequence is treated as a valid sexagenary pair. The competing Wang Guoguang hour in the earlier Wuzi catalogue remains a separate transmitted record.

[^98]: **A third imperial hour and an unchanged surname.** This Shizong record gives Jihai hour, in addition to the Jichou record and its Wuzi alternative above. The reign length and Dingmao death annotation remain as S’s statements, not certified chronology. S’s 諸大受 is romanized Zhu Dashou; G617 gives 諸大綬. The source’s impoverished man sharing that chart is retained even though G omits him.

[^99]: **Valid catalogue differences are not overwritten.** In the final group S gives the provincial administration commissioner Dinghai/Yichou and the bureau director Jiaxu/Dingchou; G617 gives Dinghai/Yisi and Jiaxu/Dinghai. S’s valid pairs are retained. The educational posts and the seventy-year lifespan in S’s final examples likewise remain, rather than being removed to match G’s shorter list.

[^100]: **Two repairs and one retained variant.** S’s short verse has 親面冬支離; G617 has 親面各支離, supporting “[each] scattered apart,” while 親面 itself remains an unusual expression. S’s 父母完全术睦 is supported by G’s 不睦, hence “[not] harmonious.” In the next exclusion S gives 壬巳甲寅, while G gives 癸巳甲寅; Ren is retained rather than changed to Gui. The prose’s need for a clash and the final song’s rejection of clash are not silently harmonized.

[^101]: **Shared charts and specified ages.** S’s Yan Qing and Ye Mengxiong captions each add a differently situated person sharing the complete chart. These comparisons are retained without equating their lives. The Renzi rule explicitly places hardship after thirty-five between early and late wealth; the number is not converted into a calculated Gregorian date. The Sha and illness assertions remain historical prognoses.

[^102]: **The source labels two accounts.** S gives Sun Piyang Renyin/Gengzi here as 原本, the original text, and Renyin/Xinchou in the next section as 星案, *Star Cases*. Both have Renchen/Jiachen year and month. The next entry adds childlessness. No preferred historical hour is manufactured from this disagreement.

[^103]: **One father’s chart, several descendants.** The Guimao/Yichou caption belongs to the honorary official Yuan. Zongdao’s metropolitan first place, the degrees of Hongdao and Zhongdao, and grandson Pengnian’s degree are family annotations, not extra birth charts. 經魁 in the preceding catalogue denotes distinction in a classics division of an examination; it is not an examiner’s office.

[^104]: **“Owl day” remains as transmitted.** S and G618 both have 梟日 in the verse; the translation does not silently change it to 梟印, Owl Seal, or add a new day class. The preceding prose clearly uses Geng as Inverted Food and Gui as Blade. The loaded-boat and following-wind images express obstruction and changing fortune, not nautical advice.

[^105]: **Names and unnamed records.** S writes 耒宗道; the source graph is preserved in this note and romanized Lei Zongdao rather than silently changed to the similar 來. The Zhu Dazhou/Secretariat-drafter comparison is retained in S’s placement, though G618 attaches a related alternative-year notice to Qin Mingxia instead. S’s final Dingyou/Xinhai pair has no caption, and remains explicitly unlabelled.

[^106]: **An impeachment repair and an unresolved caption.** S’s 効張居正 in Liu Tai’s entry is read as 劾張居正, impeaching Zhang Juzheng, supported by G618 and S’s later account of the same official. Wei Shichun’s comparison ends 一四川史, literally a Sichuan “shi”; 士, scholar, would be a plausible conjecture but is not established by G’s omission. The English leaves this descriptor uncertain rather than inventing an occupation or name.

[^107]: **Dragon and Tiger are not rewritten as a regular clash.** 龍虎相衝 is present in S and G618, even though Chen and Yin are not an ordinary six-clash pair. It is translated as written, not changed to Dragon and Dog to match the Storehouse explanation. The claim may be compressed or corrupt; the consulted witness does not settle it.

[^108]: **A repaired office and a shared fourfold chart.** S’s 正鄕 is G618’s 正卿, supporting “[court minister].” G gives a surname absent from S; it is not imported. The four Renyin pillars are expressly shared by Chief Censor Han and an immensely rich man, not duplicated accidentally. Their different attainments remain part of the catalogue.

[^109]: **Rare or doubtful names are not replaced by assumed identities.** S’s 林針 is rendered Lin Zhen, with the graph retained here; the additional case is absent from G618. S writes 胡嚴 in the following Renwu catalogue, where G has 胡儼. The English Hu Yan does not conceal that graphic difference. The source’s higher title for Liu Chuxian is also retained rather than lowered to match the shorter G caption.

[^110]: **A death date split by the case grid.** Han Bangyu’s caption has 甲 at the end of the upper row and 辰被弑 below: Jiachen is the year he was slain, not an extra natal month. The preceding Xinmao/Wuxu graduate is a separate example, supported by G618’s pairing. 弑 is rendered as a killing, without inventing the murderer or political circumstances not supplied here.

[^111]: **The title Great Void.** S reads 名白太虚, while G618 has 名曰太虛, “called Great Void.” The title follows the supported reading 曰. It is not the ordinary two-branch Void of a sexagenary round, and no calculation method for a new Void is added. The unfavorable generalization is retained beside the preceding favorable day judgments.

[^112]: **Body and Blade occupy different stages.** The prose expressly contrasts Ren’s Death at Mao with Gui’s Birth there, while Yi flourishes as Hurting Officer. The opening 傷鬼 is compressed, but need not be rewritten into an invented list of further stars. The translation preserves the injury-to-ghost language and lets the prose supply its explanation.

[^113]: **Several day–hour accounts are transmitted.** S places Liu Tai here at Renwu/Guimao and explicitly gives Renshen/Xinchou as an alternative; its earlier Xinchou section places him under Renwu. G618’s related entry appears under Renshen/Xinchou. These are not silently made into one chart. S’s 張居止 is read as Zhang Juzheng, supported by its other captions and G. The Renwu restoration and posthumous title are retained as the source’s biographical statements.

[^114]: **An uncertain name preserved.** S writes 衷頁吉, romanized Zhong Yeji, for the Nanjing minister of War. The case is absent from the consulted G618 catalogue. A repair to a better-known minister’s name would therefore be conjectural; none is silently made. The year Gengyin and month Jiashen remain clear in the source.

[^115]: **Career dates and a damaged title.** Xu Xuegu’s Guimao is a luck course and Xinsi the year of death; Dingyou/Wushen is his natal year/month pair. Another case gives only 甲部, “Jia ministry,” an uncertain title retained without assigning it to Personnel or another ministry. Wang Jiheng’s Dingchou examination and the half-month interval before the city’s loss are likewise biographical annotations.

[^116]: **An attested literary-name repair.** S has 寅多比古崇, but G618 reads 寅多比石崇. “[Shi Chong]” follows that witness: a comparison evoking conspicuous wealth, not a missing technical phase. The final image of the Azure Dragon entering its temple is kept distinct from the named Ren-Riding-the-Dragon’s-Back configuration.

[^117]: **Wu, not Xu, in the explanation.** S’s 丙戌健旺 is G618’s 丙戊健旺, supporting “Bing and [Wu] are vigorous.” The sentence is discussing Wealth and Indirect Officer, already identified as Bing and Wu. The actual repair is nevertheless supported by a witness, not merely imposed from those expected relationships.

[^118]: **A marked conjecture in a damaged anecdote.** S has 宴日工馬破卵而亡. “[Mounting] a horse” conjecturally reads 工 as 上; the consulted G618 catalogue omits the case and supplies no confirming variant. The genital injury and death are retained without graphic expansion or a modern diagnosis. Xinchou is the metropolitan examination year, separate from Renshen/Bingwu birth pillars.

[^119]: **Wood month retained against Wei month.** S says 木月, a Wood month; G618 has 未月, Wei month. S’s intelligible reading governs. The following claims about injury and self-punishment are historical prognoses, not verified consequences of the day–hour pairing.

[^120]: **Support and following are not merged.** The text speaks both of support through Metal/Water and of abandoning the original fate to follow Wealth. Those are different modes of analysis, preserved as the source presents them. Its 禄馬三奇, Three Wonders of Lu and Horse, is not replaced by an invented standard three-stem set.

[^121]: **Two month readings restored.** S’s Renwu entry has 冬日 and 丑日, winter day and Chou day, although its subject is already a Renwu day. G618 supplies 冬月 and 丑月. The English “[month]” follows those attested readings. Yuan Wei’s childlessness remains a case-caption statement beside the general favorable rules; no sons are inferred from the configuration.

[^122]: **A supported negative and a name graph.** S’s 不過刑衝破害 in the closing song is understood with G618’s 不遇, not encountering punishment, clash, breaking, or harm. This is a contextual repair to the connective, not a new exemption. S’s 史𢎞詢 is rendered Shi Hongxun, supported by G’s 史弘詢; the son’s distinction belongs to the father’s caption, not an additional chart.

[^123]: **The opening and prose differ in emphasis.** The opening says 夫化妻從, the husband transforms and the wife follows; the explanation says 夫從妻化, the husband follows the wife in transformation. Both are retained, rather than reversing either phrase to make them identical. These are the text’s relational images for the Ding–Ren transformation, not a modern rule governing spouses’ conduct. G618 also preserves the difference.

[^124]: **Heavenly Virtue, not a forecast inserted into a month list.** S reads 辰月夭月德貴; G618 has 辰月天月德貴, supporting “[Heavenly] and Monthly Virtue.” The repair is distinguished from the genuine injury-to-wife-and-children statement earlier in the entry, which remains. S’s Guisi/Xinchou censor differs from G’s Guisi/Xinyou and is not replaced.

[^125]: **The source itself finds the chart difficult.** Zhou Xian’s late examination success, good son, and four clashing pillars lead the annotator to call the chart hard to judge. That admission is translated rather than replaced by a confident retrospective explanation. 京棍 in the preceding catalogue is a derogatory description of a capital rogue or swindler, not an official post.

[^126]: **A shared chart and two career dates.** Lin Daqin’s Renchen is the year of his first-place palace examination and Dingyou the year of death. Xinwei/Gengzi remains his natal year/month pair. S expressly adds a provincial graduate with the same complete chart. Neither the second person nor the disparity of attainments is removed.

[^127]: **Later political events belong to the transmitted catalogue.** Cheng De’s death in the Chongzhen calamity and Xuan Guozhu’s appointment and demotion within his examination year are retained as S’s statements. The compiler’s Ming attribution is not a claim that every later catalogue annotation was written by him. The last 己未/丁卯 caption is only 逆奴, rebellious servant; it is not expanded into a reconstructed biography.

[^128]: **A particular day within a general-hour verse.** S and G618 give 壬戌 in the quatrain, even though the section concerns all six Ren days. Renxu is retained, not silently changed to 壬日. 紫泥宣 evokes an imperial edict with its seal; the English does not treat purple sealing clay as a remedial substance or additional natal indicator.

[^129]: **A small conjectural position reading.** S and G618 both have 酉土沐浴. The English reads “at Bath upon You,” understanding 土 as the similar graph 上 in this construction. This is an explicit conjectural interpretation, not an attested correction from G. It avoids falsely declaring the ordinary You branch to be Earth; the following sentence itself calls its Xin Metal flourishing.

[^130]: **S’s given name and title remain.** S writes 程軌, Cheng Gui; G618 has 程軏, Cheng Yue. The translation retains S’s name rather than silently identifying a preferred historical person. S also explicitly calls Gui E a provincial graduate before his grand-secretary title; that credential is not replaced by an unprinted degree.

[^131]: **Renshen restored.** S gives 壬甲 for Liu’s year, which is not a sexagenary pair. G618 has 壬申, supporting “[Renshen].” The Jiyou month and palace-first designation are retained. The concluding warning about an incorrectly fixed birth hour belongs to the source; it does not certify the catalogue’s dates.

[^132]: **A hidden stem restored.** S’s opening has 明庚暗戌, visible Geng and hidden Xu; G618 reads 明庚暗戊, supporting hidden “[Wu].” The prose immediately identifies Wu as Indirect Officer. “Demon” preserves the opening 魔 rather than silently converting it into the differently written technical ghost 鬼.

[^133]: **The source explicitly marks a gap.** 原闕 follows the Renchen judgment where a case list might be expected. It is retained as a gap notice. No chart or personal name has been invented to fill it, and G618’s absence of a catalogue there does not establish what, if anything, was lost.

[^134]: **Lu in the Lu–Horse idiom.** Ren seated at Wu is described as seated upon Lu, within 祿馬同鄉. This is not silently corrected to the ordinary Ren Lu position at Hai; the idiom uses Officer and Wealth as Lu and Horse. S gives Wuzi/Renxu for the vice minister, while G618 gives Wuzi/Wuxu. The valid S month remains.

[^135]: **An unresolved qualification retained.** Both S and G618 say 若通氣減福, if qi communicates, blessing diminishes, without specifying which qi. Elsewhere communication is favorable, but adding “Fire” or “Officer” here would be conjecture. The translation preserves the brief qualification instead of supplying a doctrinal reconciliation.

[^136]: **A name and a month differ.** S writes 李幾, rendered Li Ji, where G618 has 李璣. S gives Ling Xiang the month Gengyin, whereas G gives Gengchen. Neither the valid month nor the written name is silently replaced. The middle-life distinction and death after fifty are separate from the preceding Renyin-hour account’s warning of adversity after fifty.

[^137]: **Very different outcomes remain together.** The Duke of Lu and a person drowned at nineteen expressly share a chart. The same list includes violent deaths and Wang Xuekui’s ninety-three years. S splits the final 三 of that age onto the next line; G618 confirms 壽九十三. The posthumous punishment of Gao Guangxian is the catalogue’s report, translated without further graphic elaboration or independent biographical verification.

[^138]: **Names and a graph-supported reading.** 陳炌 is rendered Chen Kai, and 楊起元 as Yang Qiyuan, following S; the latter is 楊啟元 in G618. S’s 黄養𫎇 is supported by G’s 黃養蒙, hence Huang Yangmeng. The ordinary Nanjing man sharing Chen Kai’s chart remains a distinct outcome; no name is supplied for him.

[^139]: **Diminishing blessing and the unresolved Water phrase.** S has 暗合咸福; G618 has 暗合減福, supporting “[diminish] blessing.” Both transmit 水從金木自然強, Water following Metal and Wood and naturally strong. The English preserves both phases rather than deleting Wood because ordinary generative logic would make the phrase easier.

[^140]: **Identical romanization, different signs.** The first list is 戊、己、午、未: Wu Earth stem, Ji Earth stem, Wu Horse branch, and Wei. In the Guichou entry the corresponding list ends 巳, Si, as S and G619 both transmit. The variation is not silently corrected to Wei. Unambiguous 己/巳 graphic confusions are resolved in context without merging the signs themselves.

[^141]: **A malformed month left incomplete.** S gives 庚戊 beneath Dai Yao’s Renyin year. 戊 is a stem, not a branch; 庚戌, Gengxu, is a plausible repair, but the additional case is absent from G619 and no secure supporting witness was established. The English therefore leaves the branch uncertain rather than publishing a conjectural natal month as fact.

[^142]: **A shared flattened record, not certain individual biographies.** After Tang’s Wushen/Gengshen entry, S supplies 癸丑 above 乙丑 with captions for Chu Guoxian and Lu Shi. Guichou/Yichou is the recoverable year/month pair, but the original flattened typography does not explicitly say 命同 here. The translation exposes that attachment instead of disguising it as two independently verified charts. Guiyou/Bingxu and Xinmao/Jiachen occur with examination and office statements, not as four additional natal pillars.

[^143]: **A negative that conflicts with the familiar formula.** S and G619 both say 官不加兮財不豐, without Officer added, Wealth is not abundant. It is not silently changed to 財自豐, Wealth naturally abundant, although that would harmonize better with some Returning-Lu teachings elsewhere. The rule and the source’s own differences must be read together.

[^144]: **Treasure Vase and an imperial-image variant.** 寶瓶 is retained as Treasure Vase; the verse alone does not establish a modern tropical Aquarius placement. S ends the investiture line with 紫薇封, Purple Tenuity’s investiture, whereas G619 has 紫泥封, a purple-sealed patent. S’s celestial image remains. The final Jia/Wu/Yin/Hai list is not expanded into unprinted conditions.

[^145]: **Bing and Wu recovered twice.** S gives 丙戌 in the general explanation and the Guichou rule; G619 has 丙戊 in both. “[Wu]” is restored as the Officer stem paired with Bing Wealth. The prohibitions later distinguish Wu and Ji stems from Si, Wu, and Mao branches; they are not a sequence of complete year pillars.

[^146]: **A missing place and an unresolved alternative.** The impoverished scholar sharing Wang Jiaping’s chart is followed by 闕人 in S. The place is left missing, not guessed to be Fujian or any other province. Both S and G619 preserve 孤中失土貴; “solitary, losing land, yet noble” is a tentative punctuation of that compressed alternative, not a restored full explanation.

[^147]: **The line’s grammar remains uncertain.** S has 不過空乏難動; G619 gives 不遇空乏難動. Neither securely establishes a smooth conditional without further interpretation. The English retains S’s “not passing beyond emptiness and want” rather than presenting an unqualified auspicious rule. The key and Storehouse imagery in the surrounding lines is nevertheless clear and is translated in full.

[^148]: **A materially different opening verse.** S has 刃復官祿減精神, tentatively rendered Blade returning upon Officer and Lu; G619 reads 刃傷背祿減精神, Blade and injury, turning from Lu and diminishing the spirit. The English retains S’s words rather than silently substituting the smoother parallel. The 原闕 notice following the general explanation is also preserved.

[^149]: **A person moves between day entries in the witnesses.** S places Minister Wu Yue’s Jiazi/Renshen record under Guisi/Jiayin, while G619 places it under Guichou/Jiayin. The translation follows S’s position. In the Guimao prose, S’s 亥卯木月 is retained as Hai and Mao Wood months; G’s 亥卯未月 is not substituted merely to complete a familiar triple combination.

[^150]: **An uncertain short caption.** S gives only 撫翰 for Yiwei/Jiashen. G619 has 高撫翰, which could include a surname or appellation, but does not establish that S’s two graphs alone name a particular office. “Fuhan” is therefore left explicitly uncertain; neither an unprinted surname nor a normalized Hanlin appointment is supplied. Shen Wei’s Renshen is a death year, separate from his Jichou/Jisi natal pair.

[^151]: **Ren remains in the song.** The closing list 無壬巳戊庚申 is transmitted by both S and G619. Ren is retained even though the general explanation chiefly excludes Officer/Killings, Geng, and Shen. The source supplies no additional argument reconciling every item; none is invented. The imperial city and tiger list are images of advancement and examination success.

[^152]: **Food and Hurting Officer remain distinct labels.** The section names Yi Food God in its general account, but calls some spring-born examples Hurting-Officer configurations. Both labels stand. 巳破 and 巳土 in S’s prose are read as Ji, 己, with G619 supporting the reading; the opening Xin/Ji exclusion is understood through that explanation, not as a single Xinsi pillar.

[^153]: **A surname variant retained.** S writes 張嘉善, Zhang Jiashan, whereas G619 gives 黃嘉善, Huang Jiashan. The translation keeps S’s surname and Jimo origin. S’s 趙帥尹, rendered Zhao Shuaiyin, is absent from G’s shorter Guiwei catalogue; it is not silently normalized to a better-known name.

[^154]: **Favorable Xin and You after their earlier exclusion.** S and G619 both conclude with 甲寅辛酉頗安常, Jiayin and Xinyou bringing settled ease, although Xin and You are excluded in the opening explanation. Both passages are translated without changing the final phrase into a warning. Gui’s Birth at Mao follows the reversed yin-stage scheme used here, not the yang Water Birth at Shen.

[^155]: **An accusation, not a reconstructed examination case.** S says Shen Tonghe was metropolitan first in Bingchen and dismissed and banished for 假文, fraudulent writing. The exact mechanism—an essay, document, or other deception—is not securely specified. The English therefore avoids inventing details of how the alleged fraud occurred. G619 omits this additional case.

[^156]: **A missing branch and a second flattened shared record.** S supplies only 辛 beneath the Renyin junior administrator; the month branch remains missing. It also places Dingwei above Renzi around two captions, for 汪道亨 and 林學曾. Their Jimao/Guiwei and Renchen examination dates remain separate from the recoverable Dingwei/Renzi natal pair. Since G619 omits these additions, it cannot independently establish the attachment of each caption.

[^157]: **Xu restored in two key lines.** S’s short summary already gives 卯戌, Mao and Xu; the following quatrain and song instead have 卯戊. G619 reads 卯戌 in all three places, supporting “[Xu]” in the two damaged lines. The change is a witnessed character repair, not an inference that every clash with a Storehouse must be beneficial.

[^158]: **Repeated pairs are separate records.** Xinmao/Xinmao occurs twice in the Guiyou catalogue, once for a deputy court minister and once for 劉瑊, Liu Jian, palace second. Both are retained; neither is deduplicated. 庚戌/甲申 is labelled 乙部 in S’s Guichou catalogue, an unclear ministry designation kept as such, not treated as a new personal name.

[^159]: **Phoenix and jade share the verse.** Both S and G619 give 鳳落荊山 followed by jade hidden in stone. The phoenix is not silently changed to jade to simplify the image. 三重蛇馬, threefold Snake and Horse, is likewise preserved without adding a calculation for three named stars. The bowing beside Phoenix Pool is an image of court service, not a ritual prescribed to the reader.

[^160]: **A supported ending and an unexpanded region.** S’s 合局而貫 is G619’s 合局而貴, supporting “[distinction].” S then has 癸水地方之氣, the qi of Gui Water’s region; G specifies 北方, north. The English keeps S’s less explicit wording. Actual transformation into Fire and a strong Gui body that does not transform are different cases, not one phase identity.

[^161]: **An additional Metal-month rule retained.** S’s Guihai judgment says 未月東方運金月有制俱貴顯, rendered Wei month with eastern luck, or a Metal month with restraint. G619 instead gives 金紫 and an additional ominous alternative. The S wording is not overwritten. The last three S records—including the graduate stripped of status—are retained despite their absence from G’s shorter catalogue.

[^162]: **An internally supported hour repair, not a corrected parallel verse.** Both S and G619’s opening verse read 癸未, Guiwei. Their general prose, six day entries, closing song, and hour sequence consistently use 己未, Jiwei. The opening “[Jiwei]” is therefore an emendation supported by the section’s internal evidence; it is not falsely attributed to a different G opening. The transmitted Guiwei is recorded here.

[^163]: **Historical organ correspondences.** The kidney, bladder, channel, lower-back, knee, abscess, and growth descriptions are translated within the text’s correlative medical vocabulary, not as proven consequences of the chart. The difficult 若通月制又不通水氣 condition is retained in both witnesses; the English does not change it into a promise that restraint by itself produces high distinction.

[^164]: **Valid months differ between witnesses.** S gives the Renchen provincial graduate Bingwu month and Grand Secretary Xu’s wife Bingshen month; G619 has Gengxu and Yiwei respectively. S’s valid month pairs are retained. The wife’s chart is not presented as the grand secretary’s own. The later Wuyin/Gengyin administration commissioner likewise differs from G’s Gengshen month.

[^165]: **Chou versus Mao in the key imagery.** The quatrain names Chou and Xu, while the final song names Mao and Xu. Both sequences occur in S and G619, so neither is silently substituted for the other. The returning flowers and ranks of geese are images of offspring and siblings; no botanical or animal-based remedy is implied.

[^166]: **Visible Wealth differs from Wealth invited by combination.** The general account both obtains Wealth and Officer through Shen’s combination with Si and warns against Wealth actually present in the pillars. The translation preserves that distinction rather than deleting either occurrence of Wealth. The opening first 巳 is read 己, Ji, with G619’s 己丙寅巳 supporting the stem/branch distinction.

[^167]: **The Golden Crow and Yu’s Gate.** S’s 金烏大陽 is read as the Golden Crow and Sun, supported by G619’s 太陽. 禹門 evokes the Dragon Gate and the image of ascent to distinction; the verse does not provide a literal location as advice for travel. The final list of signs and the praise of freedom from punishment remain as transmitted.

[^168]: **An obscure livelihood phrase.** S and G619 both read 遇貴生涯, literally a livelihood of meeting the noble. “Livelihood must be sought through meeting the noble” is a contextual rendering, not an attested repair to another occupation. The sentence does not establish that support is unnecessary; its next clause explicitly welcomes support.

[^169]: **The catalogue makes a disputed family assertion.** S claims that Yan Shifan was Yan Song’s wife’s nephew rather than his son, then opposes pity for Yan Song. The translation reports this as S’s annotation; it does not certify the kinship allegation or turn the moral judgment into a chart-derived fact. G619 gives the chart but omits the extended annotation. S’s 仕丘 for Honorary Official Liu’s locality is retained as Shiqiu, explicitly uncertain, rather than silently changed to Renqiu. The Muzong reign length, age, and alternative hour also remain transmitted claims.

[^170]: **A different hour occurs only in the parallel.** G619 adds Dingsi as an alternative for Feng Bao and then argues from Water’s Defeat at You and the hour’s final result. S preserves the You explanation without the alternative; the translation does not insert the extra hour into its main text. No unrecorded bodily consequence is inferred from the fact that the caption calls him a palace eunuch.

[^171]: **The woman’s relations and a split death date.** The Gengwu/Dinghai caption identifies one woman as Grand Tutor Yang’s wife and the vice minister’s mother; Renwu is her death year. Zhang Yicheng’s 丙 is separated from 辰年卒 by the month row. Bingchen is therefore the death date, while Wuchen/Yimao remains his natal year/month pair. G619 does not supply a fuller version of these added captions.

[^172]: **Tianyi and Long-Flowing Water are not normalized away.** S and G619 both begin the quatrain 天乙壬癸, not 天干. Tianyi is retained. 長流水 in the final song is rendered Long-Flowing Water, but the passage does not spell out whether its application is the named nayin class or the broader image; no additional natal pair is inferred. The key positions Chen/Chou in the short summary and Chen/Xu in the song remain different.

[^173]: **Inviting what is absent.** Bing and Wu are said to be extinguished at Hai, yet Hai clashes them forth from Si. This is the remote-invitation logic of the named configuration, not ordinary possession of those stems in the chart. The first prohibition in S has 戊巳, read as Wu and Ji with G619’s 戊己; its later prohibition expressly concerns Si filling the vacancy. The two should not be conflated.

[^174]: **Tang Ruyi, as S writes it.** S gives 唐汝揖, rendered Tang Ruyi, while G619 has 唐汝楫, Tang Ruji. The S graph is retained rather than silently replaced by a historical identification. S’s 己亥/乙亥 magistrate is labelled 乙令, an unclear caption left qualified. S’s 李橒 is rendered Li Yun; the uncommon graph is not changed to another governor’s name.

[^175]: **Ages, sons, and career dates remain separate.** Zong Chen’s thirty-six years and childlessness, Liu Shiqiu’s short life and nine sons, and Zhou Yanru’s two entries into the Grand Secretariat are all retained. Zhou’s Guichou examination and Guiwei ordered death are not extra natal pillars. G619 omits his added caption; the translation therefore does not claim corroboration for it from that witness.

[^176]: **Winter is both welcomed and rejected in different passages.** The Guihai prose favors winter birth; the last song avoids winter. Both S and G619 preserve the difference. 戊丙庚木, Wu, Bing, Geng, and Wood, is also retained rather than replacing 木 with a guessed missing stem. The next 己亥 is read as separate Si and Hai with G619’s 巳亥; Jia and Bing then continue the list. The final verdict of early defeat and later accomplishment remains intact.

[^177]: **The transmitted text acknowledges additions and hearsay.** S expressly assigns six parts in ten of the foregoing fate records to the original and four to additions, then admits duplicate records and possible errors of report. G619 has no corresponding closing note. These proportions are the annotator’s own statement, not a ratio measured by this translation or a verified genealogy of every case. The admission is essential to reading the many competing charts and later historical captions in this juan.
